To the memory of one of my best friends 
of all time, the late Chester _A. Congdon, 


this book ts dedicated. 


OU U9 it 


Memories 


desk now in meditative work on the catalog I can see from the windows of my office 
here at the farm, groups of Rosemere cattle which, after June 21st, will be gone, who 
knows where? At any rate, they will go from under my care and anxious supervision. 


ad Ee is with a heavy heart that I go about the preparations for this sale. As I sit at my 


Somehow these cattle have grown to bealmost a part of me, so closely have we been 
associated down through the years. I remember well how, under my mother’s direction, I 
first started to work with them as a boy of twelve. Yes, and I think of all the subsequent 
years—years of labor, study and effort to make them better and how nobly they responded. 
I think of the days of restless energy which scarcely left time for sleep when I was prepar- 
ing them for the shows or other important events. And how we travelled together up and 
down and across America so many times. How I would lie awake nights feverishly think- 
ing of the next show as the fast exhibition specials with their swaying and lurching cars 
raced through the night. We made many, many shows together—some to the far corners 
of the continent—often we were successful and sometimes we lost. But win or lose, I never 
felt that the cattle let me down. They were troopers all—troopers of the tanbark. 


I can see them now as they paraded through the show rings, just as plainly as though 
yesterday were today, but if I should need a fresh reminder I would have only to look at the 
young things about me at this time which so vividly portray the characteristics passed on to 
them by these early ancestors. To those old friends I cannot give too much credit. They 
were responsible, in a large measure, for the present high standard of excellence in Rose- 
mere cattle. The great sires—Black Woodlawn, Glenfoil Thick Set 2d, Oakville Quiet Lad, 
Idolmere, Meraman of Tierra Alta, Prizemere 9th, Prizemere 32d, Blackmere 16th—all are 
gone now, but while they lived they blazed a path of glory for future generations of Rose- 
mere cattle to follow. And I wish they might know that their descendants have upheld the 
high Rosemere tradition established by them. 


Then I think again of some of the wonderful females which lent so much lustre to the 
early days of the herd. I remember how proudly I led some of them to victory in the In- 
ternational arena at Chicago and I wish they, too, could know that their names are written 
high today amongst the recorded triumphs of the breed. I seem to see them standing be- 
fore me in all their superb beauty, feminine loveliness and every other appearance of high 
breeding—Hileen Lass, Glenfoil Queen 2d, Her Majesty 2d, Queen of Rosemere 5th, Barbara 
of Rosemere 33d, Barbara of Rosemere 40th, Barbara of Rosemere 64th, and others of the 
early celebrities of the herd. They, too, are gone, but once they trod the paths that led to 
fame and they left behind them a great heritage. Their sons and daughters have carried 
to still greater heights the glory of Rosemere. 


So let me speak for a moment of some of these. After all, are they not just as dear to 
me? Who in recent years has not heard of the phenomenal Pride of Rosemere 67th, Junior 
and Reserve Grand Champion as a calf at the International of 1925, and Grand Champion at 
the same show as a yearling in 1926? She with her sisters, Pride of Rosemere 59th and 
Blackbird of Rosemere 56th, made up that famous trio of undefeated daughters of Prize- 
mere 9th which, with Pride of Rosemere 67th’s full brother, Prizemere 32d, won the first 
prize get of sire at Chicago and Philadelphia in 1926. And who will forget the triumphs of 
Barbarian of Rosemere 64th, Epponian 8th of Rosemere, and Blackcapmere 113th—cham- 
pions all, and which bid fair to be champion sires as well? And the beautiful Barbara of 
Rosemere 100th, champion three years in succession at the American Royal and Grand 
Champion with her stablemate, Barbarian of Rosemere 64th, at the International of 1933. 
Or Blackcapmere 62d, the 1929 Reserve Junior Champion bull, and Pride of Rosemere 
289th, the 1932 Junior Champion female. These and many, many others have been feted 
actors in the show rings of recent years and are living evidence of the unusual prepotency of 
the great animals that went before them in the herd. 


And must I now say “good-bye” to them all? No!—better “au revoir’, because I really 
do expect to see them again—if only in memory.

The Beginning of the Herd 
at Yakima 


By WALTER B. CONGDON 


T Otto Battles’ suggestion that it might be of some interest to Aberdeen-Angus breeders to learn 
A something about the beginning of the Congdon & Battles Herd, the following brief account is giv- 

en as an example of the appeal which the breed has for anyone who is interested in beef cattle, 
has no first hand knowledge of them, but is willing to investigate. 


To begin with, it should be understood that my father, Chester A. Congdon, was responsible for the 
establishment of the herd in Yakima. His interest in the Yakima Valley began in 1888 when he was man- 
aging a syndicate formed to deal in lands along the line of the Northern Pacific Railroad, just then 
completed. Among other enterprises, this syndicate bought an area of sage brush land a few miles west of 
the present city of Yakima, and improved it. They subdivided the area into 10 to 20 acre tracts suitable 
for raising apples and pears, and constructed an irrigation canal, planted the tracts, or at least some of 
them, to trees and started to sell them off. It is beside the main point of this story, but it is neverthe- 
less interesting to note that the canal company was organized on a basis which I am quite sure was 
unique at that time. Instead of having as its purpose the sale of irrigation water to the farmers, it 
was (and is) a mutual company with one share of stock for each irrigable acre, and the farmers own the 
canal—a share for an acre. The water appropriated from the river was sufficient so that the amount at- 
tributed to one share of stock is ample to irrigate one acre. I have been told that it is the only private ir- 
rigation canal company in the United States which has never been reorganized. 


About 1904, when the syndicate’s tracts were about all sold, my father could see his excuse for vis- 
iting Yakima disappearing. He had become very fond of the country, and he therefore bought the last 
few syndicate tracts, together with some other adjoining land and planted about 250 acres of fruit 
trees, which later grew to about 450 acres. 


It was then the custom in the Valley while the trees were young, to grow other crops in the tree rows. 
There was a wholly fallacious theory that the land was so fertile that this double cropping would work 
no injury to the trees, as it would have to stop anyway in 10 or 15 years when the trees grew up. There 
was supposed to be such an ample supply of everything a plant needs except nitrogen that the trees 
would go right ahead and produce gigantic crops indefinitely, providing the nitrogen supply was kept 
up. Experience has since demonstrated that such is not the case and that consistent fertilization is as 
necessary in Yakima as in any other good farming locality. We never carried out the double cropping 
policy to the extent that most of our neighbors did; but to supply a cover crep and to add humus and 
nitrogen to the desert soil we raised alfalfa in the orchards for years, three cuttings a year. Two cut- 
tings were made into hay, sometimes three, when the season was favorable, and the fourth crop grew 
tall enough to furnish sufficient green manure to disk in. Rather than sell off the hay my father con- 
cluded to feed it to steers and so get back some manure. 


That is the way the cattle business started on our place. Common steers, very common, were plenti- 
ful; and they could eat as much feed as any “beef critters,” but they were not pretty. After a year or 
two of steers my father concluded that he might as well get a few more pounds of beef per hundred 
pounds of feed, and looked about for some good grade cows to raise his own steers. At that time and, 
in fact, until the last few years in which the good hard licks put in by Otto Battles have begun to show 
results, about the only beef cattle you could buy in Washington were Shorthorns, that is, the only ones 
that amounted to anything. There were plenty of beef Jerseys, beef Holsteins, beef Brindles, etc. So we 
got some grade Shorthorn cows and a pure bred Shorthorn bull. Naturally enough, my father began to 
spare a little time from the orchard and watch the beef cattle. One thing led to another, and he bought 
a little adjoining acreage for feed lots, pasture, etc. His interest in the cattle continued to grow and with- 
out saying much to anyone about it, he began the investigation of the relative merits of the various beef 
breeds. He read all the authoritative books he could find on the subject, and talked to meat men and 
some stock men, but not to any pure bred men, because he was not then ready to do so. 


Starting from scratch, in this fashion, he made up his mind that the Aberdeen-Angus were the best 
beef cattle; not only from the standpoint of the beef produced, but for his purpose. He also concluded 
that inasmuch as there were no Aberdeen-Angus cattle on the Pacific Coast, he might as well start in 


with pure bred stock and be in a position to supply the demand when it came, as he was convinced would 


be the case. To get ready for this he bought about 500 acres of nearby land at a rather low price. The 
low price was the result of the fact that the land was all either sage brush desert, or water-logged and al- 


kalied on account of irrigation seepage from higher land. He then drained and leveled this land for ir- 
rigation, installed pipe lines for water supply, and erected the necessary structures for a first class breed- 
ing plant, all before he bought a single Aberdeen-Angus cow. When everything was ready my father 
and I went to lowa with Mr. Howard Smith, then Live Stock Agent for the Great Northern Railroad. To 
make a long story short, we bought about 35 Aberdeen-Angus pure breds from three or four herds, and 
made the acquaintance of Otto Battles. This was the beginning of more than an acquaintanceship, and 
Otto and my father hit it off together right away. As it worked out, Otto attended personally to the de- 
livery of the first cattle to Yakima, his first visit to the Valley. He was so much impressed by the pos- 
sibilities of the region from the point of view of the breeder of registered cattle and by the new farm 
and improvements that he suggested to my father a possible partnership. I very well remember. the 
pleasure with which my father read Otto’s first letter on the subject. He had been considering the idea 
himself, but had not reached the point of suggesting it. 


This was the summer of 1916 and the terms of the deal had been settled upon, whereby Otto would 
take charge of the new herd and eventually move his Iowa herd to Yakima, when my father died, sud- 
denly and unexpectedly in November of that year. This, of course, halted the negotiations for a partner- 
ship. The Yakima enterprise, both orchard and cattle farm, was left to four Trustees, members of the 
family, to be operated for the benefit of my mother, and the six children; and Trustees cannot go into 
partnerships. However, an agreement was drawn up between the Trustees and Otto which was designed 
to put into effect the terms agreed upon between Otto and my father. I know that all, or almost all, my 
acquaintances among the Aberdeen-Angus breeders have supposed that I am Otto’s partner and I wish 
that it were true; Otto and I concluded years ago to let it go at that because the explanation proved to 
be too long and complicated. In many respects we have been partners, because I am the Trustee who has 
given most attention to the Yakima operations, and I have spent the most time there. 


In the twenty-two years since Otto went to Yakima, there has been a most notable development of 
the farm there, not to speak of the herd. Under Otto’s very skillful management the sage brush desert 
and the alkalied and abandoned land has become about the most productive farm land I have ever seen, 
whether used to produce hay, corn, small grain, or pasture. Otto did this with land that a corn expert 
told us could not be made to produce a profitable corn crop, let alone a big one. Year after year, once 
he got into the swing of it, Otto has raised what may truly be called record crops. It is for this reason 
that we have been able to carry such a large herd, taking the acreage used into consideration. Irrigation 
costs something, of course, but its costs disappear when you remember that year after year after year you 
get your rain when you want it. 


There is surely no need of my discoursing about the herd. It has talked for itself. When we began, 
we had, of course, a considerable mixture of blood lines. We had a large plant to get into operation as 
rapidly as we could. Most of the Battles Iowa herd went to Yakima when our agreement with Otto 
was executed, and the rest went out immediately after the death of Oakville Quiet Lad. I think it is 
perfectly safe to say that there is not now an animal in the herd that does not trace back by more than 
one line to Otto’s foundation herd, and to Oakville Quiet Lad. 


The last two years have been tough ones, not only for cattle breeders but for everyone. The Trustees 
are getting along in years, and they are, for Trustees, engaged in a lot of different enterprises—some 
of them unusual enterprises for Trustees. They have, therefore, decided that the time has come when 
they ought to retire from the pure bred business and the only really practicable way seems to be to 
disperse the herd. Personally, I regret the necessity very much, and I suppose I shall be found hanging 
around shows and sales as much as ever.

COMMON DESTINATION IN THE U. S. OR CANADA, WHETHER BOUGHT BY ONE PURCHASER OR

WE WOULD SUGGEST THAT THOSE WHO PLAN TO COME TO THE SALE WRITE AT ONCE TO ONE OF 
THE ABOVE MENTIONED HOTELS FOR RESERVATIONS, STATING THAT THEY EXPECT TO ATTEND OUR 
SALE. ALL OF THESE CITIES ARE CONNECTED WITH MAQUOKETA BY PAVED HIGHWAYS. 


Sale visitors traveling by railroad should use the C. M. & St. Paul or the Chicago & Northwestern. The two near- 
est main line points are DeWitt on the Chicago & Northwestern, and Delmar on the Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul. All 
main line Chicago & Milwaukee trains stop at Delmar and practically all Chicago & Northwestern main line trains stop 
at DeWitt. Passengers on Chicago & Northwestern trains which do not stop at DeWitt should stop at Clinton—a stop- 
ping point for all Chicago & Northwestern trains. 


Sale visitors arriving by train or bus at any of these destinations or any other nearby cities are requested to wire 
O. V. Battles, collect, at Maquoketa, Iowa, in advance of the time of their arrival giving information as to the number 
in their party and the exact time they expect to arrive so that free automobile conveyance to the sale may be awaiting 
them at the time of their arrival. 


For those coming by plane—The United Air Lines advise that “The Clinton County Airport (municipal) is one mile 
east of the city of DeWitt, between the Lincoln Highway on the south and railroad on the north. The field is L-shaped, 
natural drainage, level sod, with two landing strips, 2000 by 500 feet north-south, and 1900 by 750 feet east-west. The 
cite is identifiable from the air by a directional arrow marked “28 O-C.” There are two hangars and aviation fuel avail- 


able day and night.” 


TERMS AND CONDITIONS 


Settlement must be made in cash. This being a complete closing out of the Congdon & Battles interest, we cannot 
extend credit under any circumstances. 


Every animal with known defects has been eliminated from the offering. To the best of our knowledge every one 
in the consignment is physically sound and every one of producing age is a regular producer, but the cattle will be sold 
just as they are at sale time. 


Every animal has during the last seven months at proper intervals, passed four negative blood tests and four nega- 
tive tuberculin tests. Therefore, health papers can be furnished at sale time for shipment to any point in the United 


States or any foreign country. 

Breeding dates will be announced at sale time. 

After bid off animals will be at purchaser’s risk. We will care for them, however, for a reasonable length of time 
free of charge, or until satisfactory arrangements for delivery can be made.

Comments on the Congdon & Battles 
Dispersion Sale 


Bigs URL) Gs io 


Cag (ELLE Rais Q)aN' 


1938 President of the American Aberdeen-Angus Breeders’ As- 
sociation, and noted Aberdeen-Angus breeder and exhibitor of 


Miami, Oklahoma. 


Judge Fullerton also served as President of 


the American Aberdeen-Angus Association in 1937, 1928, 1927, 


1926 and 1925. 


HE production 
of live stock 
is one of the 


major branches of 
agriculture, furnish- 
ing as it does a 
market for a large 
portion of the grain 
and forage plants 
produced in farming 
operations; besides 
the vast amount of 
grasses consumed in 
grazing. 


The production of 
beef cattle probably 
results in the utili- 
zation of more graz- 
ing land than all 
other branches of 
the live stock indus- 
try combined and in 
addition consumes 
vast quantities of 
grain; and no other branch of agriculture contrib- 
utes more to the conservation and improvement of 
the soil. 


At a time when our government is engaged in 
the promulgation of a program of soil conservation 
and improvement, it is important that we give care- 
ful thought to the part we play in this very import- 
ant program. There are many reasons why we 
should encourage the production of more and better 
live stock. Aside from a small additional investment 
of capital necessary to acquire foundation stock, it 
costs no more—and in my opinion costs less—to pro- 
duce live stock of good quality than to produce an 
inferior grade or quality. 


After we have given the matter of live stock 
production the careful thought and consideration 
that prudent and conservative judgment would re- 
quire before engaging in any branch of industry and 
have arrived at the conclusion that we want to en- 
gage in the production of beef cattle, either as a 
major or incidental activity, it is but natural that we 
first of all select the breed that will give us the 
best return on our proposed investment and that is 
best suited to our conditions. In making our initial 
selection of foundation stock we should undertake to 
go to reliable and authentic sources for our facts 
and information to be relied upon in arriving at a 
decision and selecting the breed that we want to 
engage in producing. 


I submit that the slogan selected by our worthy 


Secretary for use in breed promotion literature, “THE 
BREED WITH A RECORD”, is a most appropriate 
one and if anyone who is considering the selection of 
a breed of beef cattle will make his own investiga- 
tion from records in the office of the Secretary of 
the International Live Stock Exposition at Chicago 
or from breed association literature based upon facts 
taken from such records, and give intelligent, care- 
ful, and impartial consideration to such facts, he 
cannot escape the conclusion that the Aberdeen-An- 
gus is “THE BREED WITH A RECORD” and that 
the record it has made will be convincing that no 
mistake will be made in the founding of a herd of 
Aberdeen-Angus cattle. 


It is submitted that no breed of live stock in 
America has made more progress in recent years 
and is more widely distributed than Aberdeen-Angus 
cattle. Wherever representatives of the breed have 
had an opportunity they have proven themselves 
adapted to conditions and have rendered satisfactory 
performance and returns to their owners whether it 
be in the feed lot, on the farm or on the range and 
regardless of what the climatic conditions may be, 
as is most convincingly demonstrated by reports 
from more than twenty-five different countries in 
various parts of the world where herds have been 
established. It is results of such outstanding per- 
formances of representatives of the breed in all 
parts of the world, at the greatest live stock shows 
and expositions in competition with all other breeds 
and crosses that so convincingly establish the justi- 
fication of the slogan “THE BREED WITH A REC- 
ORD” as most fittingly adopted for our great breed. 


After a decision has been made as to the breed 
the next and one of the most important matters for 
determination is the selection of foundation animals 
and in reviewing the International records and find- 
ing therefrom “THE BREED WITH A RECORD” it 
will probably appear from these same records that 
there are also “BREEDERS WITH A RECORD”, 
and a more careful review of the records will dis- 
close that for the past three decades or more one of 
the foremost breeders and exhibitors of Aberdeen- 
Angus breeding cattle has been Mr. Otto V. Battles, 
for many years the manager of the Congdon & Bat- 
tles herd of Aberdeen-Angus cattle. Mr. Battles has 
devoted almost his entire lifetime to the task of im- 
proving the type and quality of his Aberdeen-Angus 
cattle. Perhaps no breeder in America has been 
more diligent in studying questions and practices for 
the improvement of this super breed of beef cattle. 
Under his careful supervision and management some 
of the most outstanding animals have been bred and 
developed. He is probably recognized among cattle- 
men as one of the best informed men on the subject 


a. 


of live stock genetics in this country. He has given 
the subject careful and exhaustive study and has 
made practical application of the information he has 
acquired in the breeding problems and development 
of the Congdon & Battles herd of Aberdeen-Angus 
cattle until it might reasonably be regarded as the 
most outstanding herd of the breed in America. It 
has been most thoroughly demonstrated by the win- 
nings of cattle belonging to the Congdon & Battles 
herd and of animals previously selected, developed 
and exhibited by Mr. Battles at the International 
Live Stock Exposition for thirty years or more that 
he possesses rare qualifications and ability to select 
and develop superior specimens of the breed. Cattle 
selected, developed and exhibited under his manage- 
ment and supervision have been very consistent win- 
ners of show yard awards at many of the principal 
fairs, shows and live stock expositions in America, 
thus establishing the improved type and high quality 
of cattle bred and developed under his excellent man- 
agement and supervision. By his record of perform- 
ances in the selection, breeding, development, and 
exhibiting of cattle produced under his management, 
Mr. Battles has come to be recognized international- 
ly as an outstanding authority on Aberdeen-Angus 
cattle as was most convincingly demonstrated a few 
years ago when he was invited to, and did, judge 
Aberdeen-Angus cattle at one or more of the major 
live stock shows in Great Britain. 


A few weeks ago a most interesting announce- 
ment, closely approaching a sensation, was made of 
the contemplated dispersion of all animals in the 
famous Rosemere herd of Congdon & Battles’ Aber- 
deen-Angus cattle. 


The dispersion of this outstanding herd which in 
recent years among Aberdeen-Angus breeders of 
America has come to be regarded as one of the sub- 
stantial supports of the breed, is almost universally 
regretted by those most interested in the advance- 
ment of the breed. The approaching dispersion sale 
of the Rosemere herd of Aberdeen-Angus cattle is 
perhaps one of the greatest and most important 
events in breed history in this country. It will prob- 
ably not be seriously contended that more history 
could be written about animals tracing to this herd 
than any Aberdeen-Angus herd existing on the 
North American continent today. For those con- 
sidering the founding of a herd of Aberdeen-Angus 
breeding cattle or the addition of new blood to es- 
tablished herds it is a rare opportunity to select at 
auction prices representatives from this wonderful 
herd of intensely bred Rosemere cattle. No doubt 
many outstanding breeders have spent the better 
part of a lifetime in breeding cattle without such an 
opportunity, for it is seldom that an entire herd is 
offered at the very zenith of its existence to the 
public for distribution without reservation of selec- 
ted animals. 


Animals of Rosemere breeding have in years 
past been widely distributed in this country, going 
into established herds and as foundation stock for 
the founding of new herds and have uniformly ren- 
dered a good account of themselves and reflected 


credit on the herd in which they originated and its 
owners. Many specific instances could be cited as 
proof of the foregoing statement but I believe that 
the correctness of the statement will be so generally 
accepted that reference to specific cases is unneces- 
sary and would but tend to restrict the almost uni- 
versal recognition of its correctness. However, I 
cannot refrain from referring in this connection to 
our personal experience with animals of Rosemere 
breeding that have been introduced into our own 
herd and hope the personal reference will be par- 
doned when I say that some of the most highly 
prized animals in our herd are of Rosemere origin 
and their progeny without exception have proven 
most valued additions to our breeding herd, and we 
are looking forward most hopefully and with confi- 
dence to the development of the progeny of our ani- 
mals of Rosemere breeding. 


The dispersion of a herd which the greater part 
of a lifetime has been spent in building up is such 
an unusual and extraordinary event that those in- 
terested in founding a new herd or in adding new 
blood to existing herds should enthusiastically avail 
themselves of the opportunity offered to acquire ani- 
mals at public auction that at private treaty prob- 
ably would not be offered at all unless at extrava- 
gant and what to most of us would be considered 
prohibitive prices. 


In the extended pedigrees of animals cataloged 
for this eventful sale will be found animals that have 
been renowned in the making of breed history and 
I predict that in this sale will be offered specimens 
of the breed that will likewise make further history 
for the breed and be the source from which future 
champions will be produced for there can be no ser- 
ious controversy among informed breeders that 
blood will tell. “THE BREED WITH A RECORD” 
slogan can be traced to the prepotency of the many 
champions of the breed and many of such champions 
can be traced to the Rosemere herd. 


I am looking forward to the eventful occasion of 
the Rosemere dispersion sale in anticipation of the 
enthusiastic appreciation and support of all who are 
interested in the production of better live stock and 
more especially of those directly interested in the 
production of more and better representatives of 
that great breed, the Aberdeen-Angus, “THE 
BREED WITH A RECORD”. It is to be hoped that 
those comprising the firm of Congdon & Battles will 
have such support on this occasion that after it has 
become history they will feel that their efforts ex- 
pended toward the improvement and development of 
the breed have been amply appreciated and that the, 
can look back at the record they have assisted in 
making with a degree of pride that will be a source 
of pleasure and satisfaction to them throughout the 
remainder of their lives; that they will not be sat- 
isfied in the future until they have re-established 
themselves, either individually or with new associ- 
ates, in the production and development of more 
and better Aberdeen-Angus cattle. In the expression 
of such sentiments I feel that I am voicing the sen- 
timents and good will of all the members of the 
American Aberdeen-Angus Breeders’ Association.

Former head of the Animal Husbandry Department 
of Penn State College, and Secretary of the Ameri- 
can Aberdeen-Angus Breeders’ Ass’n since 1924. 


lie is sixty years since the first registered 
Aberdeen-Angus cattle were imported into 
™ the United States by Anderson & Findlay, 
Lake Forest, Illinois. The progress made by the 
breed in the United States since that time is a record 
which taxes the stretch of imagination for a new 
breed that had to be introduced in competition with 
two long established breeds. During the early 
eighties rapid strides were made in the founding of 
pure bred herds with cattle imported from Scotland. 
Most of these earlier herds were established in the 
corn belt states, which consequently became the 
breeding ground of Aberdeen-Angus cattle. These 
early importers secured outstanding bulls and fe- 
aales from the leading herds of Scotland. The breed 
was fortunate in having as some of its early admir- 
ers keen students of animal breeding, men who 
selected females and herd bulls with great care, 
both from the point of view of breeding and indivi- 
dual merit. As a result of this careful selection of 
foundation animals a number of herds were develop- 
ed which became the fountain heads of the breed in 
America. 


This newly imported breed was early exhibited at 
the local, state and national shows to advertise the 
breed, and to the great surprise of the American 
cattlemen consistently carried off the major honors 
on their merit in interbreed competition. In the 
carcass contests, fat carloads and single steers the 
breed soon became an important winner by constant- 
ly winning more grand championships than any 
other breed. 


Among these early breeders and importers were 
Anderson & Findlay, Alex Geddes, J. J. Rodgers, J. 
V. Farrell, B. R. Pierce, H. C. Allen, L. McWhorter, 
L. H. Herrick and A. A. Armstrong of Illinois; A. B. 
Mathews, Estill & Elliott and Gudgel & Simpson of 
Missouri; J. S. & W. R. Goodwin, W. M. D. Lee and 
Parker Parrish and Miller of Kansas; J. J. Hill, 
Alex Campbell, John C. Christie and J. Tyson of 
Minnesota; Leslie & Burwell of Wisconsin; Turling- 
ton W. Harvey of Nebraska; D. Bradfute & Son of 
Ohio; W. A. McHenry; Charles Escher & Son, W. B. 
Seeley & Son and P. J. Donohoe of Iowa. All of 
these breeders made notable contributions to the 
development of the breed prior to 1900. 


The first period up to 1900 was a period of intro- 
duction and acceptance and the period following one 
of continued improvement and expansion. Many 


noted herds and individuals have been developed 
during the period since 1900, and great improve- 
ment has been made in the breed. This progress 
was made possible because of the splendid founda- 
tion work that was done by these early breeders. 


The first important sire used by Mr. Battles was 
Glenfoil Thick Set 2d 88142, a grandson of Black 
Woodlawn, whose dam was Dolly Copeland 42389 
and sired by Brookside Star 63486. This bull was 
purchased in 1906 from P. J. Donohoe. The next 
bull of importance to be used was Oakville Quiet Lad 
109220. He was bred by John Cash, Jr., of Parnell, 
Iowa. This bull was a son of Black Woodlawn and 
his dam was Queen McHenry 47th 61884. Glenfoil 
Thick Set 2d was three times grand champion at the 
International, winning this honor in 1907, 1908 and 
1909. Oakville Quiet Lad was the International 
grand champion in 1910. The selection of these 
bulls as International grand champions not only re- 
flected the fitting ability of Mr. Battles as a judge 
of good live stock but his ability to pick winners. 
The females for the herd were as carefully selected 
as the herd bulls, hence the combination necessary 
for a great and constructive breeding program. 


The Rosemere herd of Aberdeen-Angus has played 
an important role in contributing to the expansion 
of the breed, first at Maquoketa, Iowa, under the 
ownership of Mr. Battles and during the past twenty 
years at Yakima, Washington. Mr. Battles made 
splendid progress in breeding Aberdeen-Angus cattle 
while operating single handed prior to 1916. His 
greatest work however, was done after forming a 
partnership with Mr. Congdon in 1916. At that 
time the nucleus or foundation of the present herd 
was made up of a selection of cattle from Rosemere 
and a few other leading herds in Iowa. The for- 
mation of this partnership offered a greater oppor- 
tunity for herd development because of a larger 
number of cattle and greater facilities. Mr. Battles 
has never practiced hap-hazard methods of breeding 
and mating. Records show that a definite plan of 
operation was followed at all times. The mating 
of the cattle in the herd was always given the most 
careful consideration. All important matings were 
definitely planned and based upon the principles of 
genetics. 


Weaknesses or undesirable qualities were elimin- 
ated by mating. Line breeding was followed to a very 
considerable extent with a very few out-crosses per- 


10 


mitted. This resulted in the establishment of a uni- 
form type of cattle of the ideal Aberdeen-Angus type 
that met the demand of a select trade. With a large 
number of herd bulls available it was possible to 
make matings that could not have been followed in 
small herds. Outstanding herd bulls were always 
used and proven sires always remained in the herd. 


_ The problem of selling surplus cattle was admira- 
bly met through holding extensive advertised annual 


auctions and by private treaty. In selling so many 
cattle there is certain to be some cases of animals 


not being entirely satisfactory for one reason or 
another. This firm operated on the theory that the 
customer is always right even though such was not 
always the case. Every adjustment asked for has 
always been taken care of in a satisfactory manner 
and has resulted in many repeat orders from these 
old customers and many new ones. The writer, as 
secretary, has yet to hear the first complaint or 
find the first person who was not satisfied with an 
adjustment made for cattle that did not prove satis- 
factory. A splendid record for a firm selling so 
many cattle. 


The Rosemere herd has been a potent force in the 
development of the Aberdeen-Angus industry on the 
West Coast. Many of the pure bred herds were 
started with foundation cattle from this establish- 
ment. Hundreds of Aberdeen-Angus bulls were sold 
to range owners all along the Pacific Coast and in 
the Northwest, all of which have created a greater 
interest in Aberdeen-Angus cattle. There is no herd 
in America that has enjoyed a wider range of trade 
territory than Rosemere. Cattle have been sold to 
customers in practically every state, Canada and 
Hawaii and New Zealand. 


There is no herd that has been more extensively 
exhibited. The herd has been represented at nearly 
all the leading shows each year. In recent years 
two and three show herds have been fitted and ex- 
hibited in order that the breed might be represented 
in the East, Middlewest and West. Rosemere Aber- 


deen-Angus hold the record for continuous showing 
at the International Live Stock Exposition where 
they have been exhibited continuously since 1906. 
By showing each year, this herd has an unparalleled 
record and has been an invaluable factor in popular- 
izing the breed. Rosemere also has the distinction of 
having exhibited more grand champion Aberdeen- 
Angus breeding cattle at the International than any 
other exhibitor, present or past. The showing of the 
Rosemere herd has not been limited to breeding 
cattle. Many steers have been fitted for the single 
classes at the leading shows and for carload lots at 
the Pacific Coast shows, winning numerous cham- 
pionships, especially at the West Coast shows. Many 
outstanding steers exhibited by colleges have been 
ot Rosemere breeding. 


The dispersal sale of this great herd is a distinct 
loss to the breed and the pure bred industry. On 
the brighter side of the picture, however, there is 
great satisfaction in knowing that seed stock from 
this breeding establishment, through this dispersal 
sale, will go into the herds of the East, West, North 
and South where the radiating influence of Rosemere 
breeding will be felt for many years to come. At 
this sale, foundation breeding animals _ produced 
through years of constructive breeding will be avail- 
able to all interested breeders. It will afford an op- 
portunity to purchase some outstanding breeding 
cattle that have never been offered for sale and 
would not be offered now if the herd were to ke kept 
intact. This sale should prove to be one of the most 
outstanding events in the history of American Aber- 
deen-Angus. No sale of this magnitude has ever 
been held. There will be offered for sale the larg- 
est number of outstanding Aberdeen-Angus breed- 
ing cattle that the American public has ever had 
an opportunity to buy. This sale should bring to- 
gether the largest number of Aberdeen-Angus breed- 
ers and admirers that ever assembled to an Aber- 
deen-Angus event in America. While Mr. Battles’ 
plans are somewhat uncertain we all hope he will 
continue his work with Aberdeen-Angus cattle.

a 


farm located in a land of tall corn and blue 

grass; a land of good husbandry and happy 
homes; a land where the golden hooves of well-bred 
live stock perpetuate the native fertility of the 
prairie soil. Here many years ago, a man named 
Battles practiced cattle feeding as a part of his 
farming program. He had a son, Otto, who was left 
fatherless at the age of twelve. The lad loved to 
watch the steers wax fat on the liberal rations of 
grain which were served, and he had noticed that 
among them there appeared an occasional animal 
minus horns and wearing a black hide. Otto took a 
liking to these smooth blacks and had been told that 
they were of a breed for which Iowa was even then 
becoming famous, the Aberdeen-Angus. 

The widow carried on after the master’s passing, 
and the son, young though he was, took a deep in- 
terest in all that was going on. Shortly after his 
father’s death he induced his mother to buy a pair 
of pure bred Aberdeen-Angus cows, and he main- 
tained this keen interest in the blacks until, when he 
was twenty one, he rented the farm and started on 
his own. He knew right away what he was going 
to do. One of his first resolves was to continue 
breeding the black, hornless cattle. Before begin- 
ning he had made a careful study of various herds 
prominent at that time in order to make up his mind 
what sort of a foundation he would build upon. In- 
cidentally he was also determined to learn all he 
could of the background of the breed as a whole. 
There was not so much information available then 
as now, but he knew that the story had its begin- 
nings in the north of Scotland where Wm. McCom- 
bie, Hugh Watson, Sir George MacPherson Grant, 
and their contemporaries perfected the type we 
know today. In imagination he saw the granite hills 
of Aberdeenshire, the vale of Strathmore, rich in 
song and story, and Speyside with its high and 
heathered slopes—fit settings in the motherland for 
the birth of such a “bonny” breed. He had heard 
somewhere of the long voyage of the old sailing ship 
Alabama from Glasgow to New Orleans in the spring 
of 1878, with those precious four black bulls con- 
signed to George Grant, Victoria, Kansas; the long 
journey up the Mississippi to St. Louis; the removal 
of the long horns from the old Texas cows of that 
period when the much-travelled bulls were set to 
work in Kansas pastures—probably the most stirring 
single epic in the literature of American animal hus- 
bandry. Since that remote date a vast panorama, 
sixty years in the making, has been unrolled—a mov- 
ing picture of the American agricultural progress 
presented in which the Aberdeen-Angus cattle oc- 
cupy a conspicuous place. 

Possibly the first American to interest himself in 
the introduction of Aberdeen-Angus cattle to the 
stockmen of the States was a New York City merch- 
ant, Thomas R. Clark, of the big merchandising firm 
of Fisk, Clark & Flagg. He owned a large tract of 
land near Geo. Grant’s place, and is said to have had 
an interest in the four bulls constituting the original 
importation. At any rate he was a keen observer 
of the improvement wrought at once by the black 
bulls upon the old Spanish Long Horns, and he mar- 
keted two-year-old half-bred steers of his own at 
Kansas City in the late seventies. These at once

From Farm Boy to Kame 


The Possibilities of Animal Husbandry as I Mlustrated in 
the Case of Otto V. Battles. 
By ALVIN H. SANDERS 


The dean of agricultural journalism. Former ed- 
itor and publisher of the old Breeders’ Gazette. 


attracted marked attention from the feeders of Mis- 
souri and adjacent territory and when those two Ab- 
erdonians—Thomas Anderson and George Findlay of 
Lake Forest, Illinois—made an importation from 
Scotland consisting of one bull and five females in 
the summer of 1878, the foundation of our subse- 
quent great polled Aberdeen-Angus industry had 
been duly laid. These cattle and their progeny were 
shown with success at the state fairs of that period 
and the firm made further importations. 

John V. Farwell, wealthy Chicago dry goods 
merchant, owner of a good farm in Iowa, was George 
Findlay’s employer and soon became an admirer of 
the blacks. He decided to try them out in lowa and 
in 1881 participated in making an importation of 
eighty head. Mr. Farwell, in conjunction with oth- 
ers, next proceeded to put polled bulls in service on 
the great XIT—the so-called Capitol Syndicate 
Ranch in Texas. The first black bull sold by Ander- 
son & Findlay for use on the range went to Lee & 
Reynolds, extensive operators of that period. This 
bull was so very, very black that he was facetiously 
named Snowball. Alex Geddes, a Scotchman prom- 
inent in the Chicago grain trade, brought out purely 
bred polls for use on his own farm, and thus became 
one of the pioneer supporters of the breed in the 
middle west. 

Interest was now spreading in all directions. In 
New York State, Frank B. Redfield began importing 
freely, and it was with one of his bulls that Estill & 
Elliott of Missouri—afterwards towering figures in 
the trade—made their beginning. The next history- 
making event in the corn-belt was the entrance of 
Gudgell & Simpson into the black cattle breeding 
ranks. Old Governor Simpson went to Scotland and 
the breeders over there were much impressed by the 
keeness of his judgments. In 1882 he brought out, 
among other good ones, the afterwards celebrated . 
bull, Knight of St. Patrick, and the history-making, 
Blackcap, bred by Sir George Macpherson Grant at 
Ballindalloch. These two animals contributed large- 
ly towards the growing popularity of the polls this 
side the sea. Speaking of Blackcap and Ballindal- 
loch, the writer will not soon forget a day he once 
spent as the guest of Sir George. As we strolled 
through the pastures and studied the different ani- 
mals led out for inspection from the “byres”, I was 
deeply impressed by the exceptionally fine finish, the 
superb quality of the cattle that had been produced 
in this far-famed herd. The blockiness—the sub- 
stance—of the old McCombie-Watson stock had been 
preserved and at the same time decidedly refined. 

Geary Bros., of London, Ontario; and Hon. M. H. 
Cochrane, of Hillhurst, Quebec, now began importing 
blacks into Canada. A. B. Mathews, G. W. Henry 
and others joined the early Aberdeen-Angus parade 
in the Kansas City territory. Turlington W. Harvey 
of Chicago established a great herd at his farm in 
eastern Nebraska, and in 1883 the Gearys brought 
out from Scotland the grand bullock, Black Prince, a 
Smithfield prize winner, and shipped him by express 
at large expense from Quebec quarantine to Kansas 
City in order to show him at the Fat Stock Show of 
that year at that point. He was an old time big one 
weighing 2360 pounds but smooth and richly fleshed. 
Nevertheless, he was not appreciated. The Short- 
horn power at that time was the dominating influ- 


Page 12 


Koes 


ence but the Aberdeen-Angus day was close at hand. 
J. J. Hill, “the empire builder” joined the forces pre- 
senting to American feeders the claims of the horn- 
less North Country cattle at the Chicago Fat Stock 
Show. At Kansas City in 1885, Gudgell & Simpson’s 
“Sandy”, a steer sired by Knight of St. Patrick and 
the cross-bred Angus-Hereford heifer, Burleigh’s 
Pride, were exhibited successfully. In 1887 Wal- 
lace Estill, one of the outstanding cattle breeders 
of his time in the middle west, set up the “baby 
beef” standard by showing the pony-built ‘Dot’ 
which John Imboden bought and fed to the grand 
championship of 1888. The big steer’s sun had def- 
initely set. A veritable tidal wave of enthusiasm for 
the blacks was obviously coming in. Indiana and 
Ohio breeders joined with men like B. R. Pierce and 
J. J. Rodgers of Illinois in sending well-fitted herds 
into leading show yards. The names of L. McWhor- 
ter, the Bradfutes, L. H. Kerrick, Goodwin & Judy, 
and many of like prominence became conspicuous in 
the great advance; and speaking of the “great ad- 
vance”, we find another high spot in the American 
Aberdeen-Angus story in the appearance of the 
great black bullock of that name, which Stanley 
Pierce led through to the grand championship at the 
initial International Live Stock Exposition. Then 
from the Pierces, too, came the greatest sire of the 
breed of his time, Black Woodlawn, to gain cham- 
pionship honors at Chicago. Writing of this bull 
some years ago I said: “The blood of Black Wood- 
lawn has probably been intensified in more breeds 
and animals than that of any other one bull in the 
history of Aberdeen-Angus cattle breeding in Amer- 
ica. He seems to have founded a family of his own, 
far-reaching in its beneficial results’. It was with 
this blood that Otto Battles, ambitious young Iowa 
farmer, decided—wisely as it so turned out—to be- 
gin his career as a breeder of black cattle. 


In 1887 W. A. McHenry, banker and land specula- 
tor at Denison, Iowa, founded a herd that made plen- 
ty of American Aberdeen-Angus history. His first 
marked success was with the bull Abactor by Ermine 
Bearer out of Imp. Coquette 10th—both bred at Bal- 
lindalloch. In 1892 the McHenry show herd made a 
triumphal tour of the mid-west fair circuit. Those 
were the days of inter-breed championship contests; 
and at the Illinois State Fair Mr. McHenry not only 
won in the herd competition over a grand lot of 
Shorthorns and Herefords, but his great Progress of 
Turlington was declared the best female of any beef 
breed in the show. By this time everybody recog- 
nized the rare beauty and great substance of the 
blacks. There has not been a day during the past 
forty years that they have not commanded through- 
out the entire United States the admiration of pro- 
ducers and top prices from packers. Mr. McHenry 
made a great “killing” at the World’s Columbian 
Exposition, one of the most memorable cattle shows 
ever held in America; and for a long series of years 
he contributed largely to the strengthening of lead- 
ing western herds. Wallace Estill was a great rival 
always for show-yard honors and for the patronage 
of the best black cattle breeders. Mr. Estill’s herd 
was dispersed at Chicago in March 1900. Mr. Mc- 
Henry was present, prepared to bid for show cattle, 
and arranged in advance for Bob Johnson, the Estill 
herdsman, to carry them on for the fall fairs of that 
year. There was keen competition for the best ones 
and McHenry had to pay $2,800.00 for Lucia Estill. 


The McHenry herd was sold at length to that suc- 
cessful practical cattleman, Charles Escher, Jr., of 
Botna, Iowa, for $80,000.00. This was one of the 
most important transactions of its kind ever con- 
sumated. The herd included probably the greatest 
lot of breeding matrons in this country at that date. 
Also the bull Earl Marshall, that became easily the 
greatest sire of his time; his most valuable single 
contribution to the breed being the celebrated bull 
Blackcap Revolution, for which Mr. H. O. Harrison 
of San Francisco paid James D. McGregor $15,000.00 
after winning the International Grand Championship 
of 1923. 


_ While all this and other various events of major 
importance—too numerous to even mention here— 
were being recorded, the breed was gaining new 
friends every day. Trade was particularly active in 
the state of Iowa. Escher & Ryan, the Messrs. Ev- 
ans, Pat Donohoe and many others were carrying 
the black standard to still greater heights; and pres- 
ently came an opportunity—which the Aberdeen- 
Angus fraternity quickly seized—for establishing the 
breed still more firmly in the good esteem of ieed- 
ers and killers. We refer to the founding of the Chi- 
cago International Show of 1900. Here was offered 
the chance to demonstrate in a broad way, and in the 
lime-light of fullest possible publicity, the real su- 
premacy of the Aberdeen-Angus as producers of 
prime marbled beef. The breed Association prompt- 
ly appropriated money for special prizes to exhibi- 
tors, and the big fight was on. The International 
management inaugurated at once the practice of 
bringing over from Great Britain a cattle judge of 
proved ability and one who could not possibly have 
any personal axes to grind in this competition. At 
the first show-down between the breeds at Chicago, 
the judge was one of England’s best known experts, 
Mr. J. B. Ellis of Walsingham, and when he reached 
the grand championship he found two “blackskins” 
tied for the highest honor of the year, towit: the 
champion two year old, Good Times, and the cham- 
pion yearling, Advance. After comparing them care- 
fully Mr. Ellis sent the pair for a walk together 
about the ring, and when they resumed their places 
Stanley Pierce was handed the purple rosette on Ad- 
vance. At that same show ten different lots were 
exhibited from the XIT ranch, including Shorthorns, 
Herefords, and Aberdeen-Angus, and a load of the 
latter won the feeder championship. L. H. Kerrick 
won the grand car-load championship for 15 grain- 
fed cattle on a load of polls averaging 1492 lbs. that 
sold for $15.50 per cwt. while the range for prime 
steers in the general market that week was $6.00 to 
$6.30. This “clean-up” proved but the fore-runner 
of a long series of Aberdeen-Angus International 
victories. They dropped the 1901 championship to 
a Hereford, but did they come back the following 
year? James Biggar of Scotland—the father of 
Walter, who later on served the show for twelve suc- 
cessive years—tied the ribbons in the individual bul- 
lock division. When the curtain fell that week the 
“doddies” had won two out of the three champion- 
ships by ages, grand championship with “Shamrock” 
and grand champion and reserve for best group. In 
car lots the blacks made almost as clean a sweep; 
while on the block they took five top prizes. The 
Iowa College herd supplied some marvelous mater- 
ial for this great demonstration and Charles Escher 
had the champion car load. 


As has already been intimated Otto Battles was 
tremendously impressed by the great Blackbird bull 
Black Woodlawn. His get were coming along in 
sensational fashion and were not easy to buy. Mr. 
Battles succeeded however in inducing Pat Donohoe 
to let him have two Black Woodlawn heifers and a 
young bull; but the latter did not develop to suit the 
exacting taste of the buyer, and in his stead a grand- 
son of Black Woodlawn called Glenfoil Thick Set 2d 
was obtained from Mr. Donohoe after he had won 
the junior championship at Chicago. This was in 
the fall of 1906. The following year a show herd 
was put on the road, with Glenfoil Thick Set 2d at 
the head. He was then two years old and bore away 
the International Grand Championship, and that 
same honor among females was carried by his half- 
sister, Glenfoil Queen 2d. The herd prize was won 
everywhere, including Chicago. Strange to relate, 
this extraordinary success was duplicated in 1908; 
the female grand championship this time being won 
by the two year old, Her Majesty 2d. It thus seemed 
fairly apparent that no mistake had been made in 
tying up to the Black Woodlawn blood; and it was 
thereafter steadfastly employed. 


In the spring of 1908 the Black Woodlawn bull, 
Oakville Quiet Lad, was bought. He headed his class 


Page 13 


at the shows of that year. In 1909 Glenfoil Thick 
Set 2d was sold to Mr. McHenry to head his show 
string and was again grand champion at the Inter- 
national. Meantime Quiet Lad had been placed at 
the head of the herd; and after a triumphant career 
of three years on the big circuits, concluding with 
the Chicago Grand Championship of 1910, he was 
retired from the show-yard and put in active service. 
It should be stated that in public competition he was 
never defeated. Freely used until 1920, Oakville 
Quiet Lad sired most of the star performers of Mr. 
Battles’ show herds of that period. Possibly his 
most outstanding son was Idolmere that topped all 
black bulls of 1919. While Mr. Battles was thus 
early breaking into the ranks of breed leadership, 
he was quietly adding the best animals he could buy 
from Mr. McHenry, E. T. Davis, of Iowa City, and 
the Donohoes. There were not so many of these but 
they were all strictly Class A cattle, and from them 
and their descendants the new world-famous Rose- 
mere herd was largely builded.

But coming down to the Rosemere show cattle of 
the more recent past, we find that in 1922 Barbara 
of Rosemere 33d was first and Junior Champion Fe- 
male at Chicago with her stablemate, Blackmere 
16th, which was Junior Champion Bull at the same 
show. In 1924 Barbara of Rosemere 40th was Junior 
Champion at Chicago and in 1925 Pride of Rosemere 
67th by Prizemere 9th was Junior and Reserve 
Grand Champion Female at Chicago, winning the 
grand championship the following year at the same 
show. In 1928 Barbara of Rosemere 64th by Prize- 
mere 32d was Junior and Reserve Grand Champion 
Female at the Chicago International and in 1929 
Blackcapmere 62d, also by Prizemere 32d, was Re- 
serve Junior Champion Bull. In 1932 Prizemere 
141st reappeared at Chicago after an absence of two 
years, and gained second in a class of eight aged 
bulls. He, too, was a son of Prizemere 32d. A 
daughter of that same sire, Pride of Rosemere 289th, 
was Junior Champion Female at the Chicago Inter- 
national of 19382. John C. Burns judged the class at 
the International of 1933 and in aged bulls placed 
Prizemere 141st at the top over Elliott Brown’s 
grand champion of 1932, Envious Marshall. The 
two year old bull winner in 1933 was the smooth 
deep Barbarian of Rosemere 64th which was subse- 
quently made Grand Champion. In two year old 
heifers, Barbara of Rosemere 100th, by Prizemere 
32d, headed a string of eight and walked her way 
through to the grand championship. The herd then 
went out and defeated nine competing groups, the 
pick of the show cattle of the year. 


At the so-called Jubilee Show at the International 
of 1934 Judge Fullerton of Miami, Oklahoma, whose 
herd has attained nation-wide fame, was first in ag-

senior and grand champion bull.

The Value of a Long 


Time 


Breeding Plan 


By COL. EDWARD N. WENTWORTH 


Director 
Armour’s Live Stock Bureau 


Wis Sh NONI DIS: int 


Congdon & Battles cattle will regret the 

breaking up of a herd whose history covers 
more than three decades in the life of the junior 
partner and direct manager, Otto V. Battles. The 
opportunity of bidding on the better known breeding 
cows and the tried sires of Rosemere means much to 
the majority attending this distinctive event in the 
American history of Aberdeen-Angus, but those who 
have the welfare of the breed at heart cannot escape 
a feeling of sorrow that identity of this herd can no 
longer be maintained. 

Few herds have enjoyed the advantages vouch- 
safed Rosemere. The financial backing of the senior 
Mr. Congdon, his long time vision, and his apprecia- 
tion of the necessity of numbers in the breeding 
herd if real improvement was to be secured, provid- 
ed an unusual opportunity. The continuance of his 
policies by his sons provided adequate time for con- 
structive results. The experience and skill of Mr. 
Battles, his success as a breeder of show cattle, and 
the further success of animals of his breeding in 
other herds made the combination of forces an as- 
sured triumph. 

From the start, individual merit of the animals 
used for breeding provided the chief basis of selec- 
tion, but the skill with which the animals were chos- 
en for blood lines, in establishing the herd, provid- 
ed a pedigree foundation that permitted the con- 
centration of blood to increase as selection for in- 
dividual merit progressed. The value of the Rose- 
mere herd developed because it has concentrated, 
by mild degrees of line breeding, the blood of fa- 
mous animals, so that each generation both in av- 
erage and in tops has advanced a little over the 
generation that preceded it. Cattle purchased out- 
side the herd have been selected first of all with 
the idea that their good qualities could contribute 
to the herd, but every animal has also been descend- 
ed from ancestors which were of Rosemere breed- 
ing or had been used in producing the foundation 
animals of the herd. Outcrosses have been few and 
when they have occurred they have come through 
the female line so as to produce the least disruption 
of the breeding program in case the outcross was 
not successful. So carefuliy has the breeding been 
planned that in only two or three instances have 
such outcrosses equalled the established blood lines. 

During the life of the Rosemere Herd there has 
been the greatest advance in the knowledge of 
heredity, principles of breeding, genetics, physiology 
of reproduction, animal disease, and animal nutri- 
tion that has occurred in any period of world his- 
tory. The owners of the Rosemere Herd have been 
particularly open-minded in applying practically 
what could be used of these newer advances in 
knowledge. The system under which the cattle were 
kept at Yakima has done much to test the efficiency 
of the members of the herd as feed consumers, and 
the “good doers,” both in the cows and the calves, 


(6 Feo attending the dispersion of the 


CHICAGO, ILLINOIS 


have proved themselves on the grasses of the ir- 
rigated pastures there available. Probably no herd 
of equal prominence and size has had the opportuni- 
ty of demonstrating its capacities on rations so lim- 
ited in concentrates, and persons securing animals 
from the Rosemere dispersion can feel thoroughly 
assured that these animals will utilize their feeds 
most efficiently. If beef consumption in the United 
States is to return to the volume per capita that once 
characterized it, it must be sold at prices the public 
can afford, and hence must be based on lower costs 
of production and the making of quality beef from 
cheaper food sources. Rosemere cattle have been 
tried by this standard, and their efficient perform- 
ance can almost be guaranteed. 

Proof of the value of the blood lines is not lack- 
ing as a result of show records. There is no point 
in reviewing Rosemere winnings in the show ring, 
yet five grand champion bulls and five grand cham- 
pion cows at the International Live Stock Exposition 
climax a long list of winnings; and of these, three 
bulls and two cows were calved in the Rosemere 
Herd as a result of the plan of breeding. In addition, 
ten junior or reserve grand champions were so pro- 
duced and a large number of first prize animals. 

It is beside the point to argue whether showyard 
winnings are the best measure of success for breed- 
ers. As matters stand today, they provide practically 
the only measure, since the production, exhibition and 
slaughter of pure bred steers is likely to be unprof- 
itable due to the difficulty of holding steer compe- 
titions at the time of year when market prices are 
normally most favorable. A challenge to breeders of 
beef cattle still remains unanswered in so far as 
measuring the value of their pure bred bulls and 
cows as producers of prime beef is concerned. Yet 
the Rosemere record in this particular stands close 
examination and championships or reserves have 
been won in all of the leading steer competitions of 
the country. 

One always experiences a feeling of sadness when 
good things come to an end, and the Congdon & 
Battles dispersion is no exception to the rule. The 
herd has been intimately identified with breed prog- 
ress and the events of breed history throughout its 
most trying times—under the stress of the World 
War, in the great boom that came on the heels of 
the war, and throughout the greater depression that 
trailed thereafter. Throughout the entire period the 
owners have maintained their ideals, followed their 
plan of breeding, exhibited at the leading shows, 
done their part in keeping the breed before the pub- 
lic, and in every way acted for the greater good of 
the Aberdeen-Angus and of pure bred beef cattle as 
well as for their own herd. The fraternity of breed- 
ers will miss them, and the students of constructive 
breeding will mourn their departure. All will earnest- 
ly hope that several will take advantage of this dis- 
persion to secure a nucleus of Rosemere blood on 
which many herds of the future may be built.

The Beef Cattle 


Industry 


in California 


By NELSON R. CROW, Editor and Publisher 


Western Live Stock Journal 
Los Angeles, California 


ing place in California and other far west- 

ern states. Range cattlemen are eager to 
improve the usefulness and quality of their out- 
put, and at the same time, increase the efficiency 
of production. Back of this desire is the prime 
necessity of increasing gross cash income in the 
face of restriction of the use of public lands, the 
turning over of rich valley pastures to specialized, 
cultivated crops, high land values, increased taxes 
and higher operating expense generally. More 
beef must ke manufactured on less land, and less 
desirable land, too. 


A sound beef improvement program is tak- 


I have seen the transition from a strictly range 
production proposition to a range and feedlot 
cattle business. There are cattlemen now living 
and active who recall when California was en- 
gaged in the cattle business on a large scale 
primarily for the production of hides and tallow. 
For California has been a BIG cattle state for 
more than 100 years; it is a bigger cattle state 
now than ever before; and it always will be a 
big cattle state. For out of some 114 million acres 
of land in the state, less than 10% is cultivated 
or subject to cultivation. Most of the balance 
is what we term range land—and in spite of the 
tremendous growth in population, it must con- 
tinue primarily as a range cattle state. 


Accurate estimates on cattle population go back 
only to about 1869 when California had a cattle 
population of around 1,000,000. Today, we have 
about 2,300,000 cattle, according to the United 
States Department of Agriculture. It has been 
only within the past twelve or fifteen years that 
our California cattle business was anything but 
a range proposition. Our cattle people depended 
entirely upon the elements for beef. If we were 
short of rain, the cattle didn’t get fat and there 
are plenty of instances when a great many cattle 
starved to death. 


With the increase in population, our cattle peo- 
ple gradually have had the benefit of a growing 
home market for fully 50 percent more beef than 


is being produced in California and an insistent 
demand for a year around supply of good beef. 
The tremendous influx of population from other 
parts of the United States, folk who had always 
been accustomed to dry-lot beef, demand a con- 
stantly improved quality of beef. Our cattlemen 
have found that it pays well to cater to this qual- 
ity demand—therefore the transition from a 
strictly grass proposition to feedlot finishing. 
About fifteen years ago, first efforts were di- 
rected towards supplemental feeding of cattle 
on the range. This served to prevent costly shrink- 
age in weight during the periods of short grass 
feed supplies, and at the same time, to spread 
out the marketing season from grass areas. Next 
came the feedlot finishing of cattle, so that Cali- 
fornia and adjoining states could provide con- 
sumers with a constant supply of high quality 
beef, every week in the year. 


A few years ago, to the best of my knowledge, 
we had less than half a dozen feedlots in the 
entire state. Today, California is one of the big 
cattle feeding states, and our neighbor, Arizona, 
also has gone into feed lot finishing on a big 
scale. In the two states, there were more than 
250,000 cattle in dry lots on January 1, 1938. Dur- 
ing the course of the year, it may be estimated 
that there are well above 500,000 cattle finished 
in dry lots, largely on home grown rations in- 
cluding barley, alfalfa hay, cottonseed cake, milo, 
silage and a dozen or more industrial by-products. 
We are producing beef that grades choice to 
prime under U. S. Bureau of Agricultural Eco- 
nomics grading on these rations and the Univer- 
sity of California has won the grand champion 
steer award three times at the Chicago Interna- 
tional on essentially the same rations, a fact 
which proves that corn is not essential to the ec- 
onomical production of prime beef, I might also 
mention that our feeding rations include such 
products as cull figs, raisins, dried beet pulp, 
dried grape pulp, dried orange pulp. Probably our 
cheapest and best feed is Hawaiian cane molasses 
which is available in tremendous quantity at 


Page 16 


around $12.00 a ton in tank car lots at San Fran- 
ciscoi and Los Angeles harbors. I doubt if there 
is any other section of the United States where 
good and choice grades of beef can be produced 
more economically, or on such a great variety of 
rations. 


To a great extent, it is this feed lot develop- 
ment that is back of the demand for good range 
bulls. Grass is the most economical feed in the 
world and when ill-pred cattle waste this grand 
feed, the cattleman is not so likely to take note 
of the waste. But when you put cattle into a 
feed lot, it is always expensive, whether in Iowa 
or California. Your figures on costs of produc- 
tion quickly show that well-bred feeder calves, 
with their natural easy {fleshing qualities, util- 
ize costly grain rations and other concentrates, 
more quickly and more economically than do the 
mediocre kinds. And when the supreme test comes 
at marketing time, there is usually a substantial 
premium in price because of the better quality 
product. 


Out here in our country, range men are whole- 
sale buyers of really good stock bulls. In fact, 
there is no practical limit to the number of gen- 
uinely good, beef type bulls that can be sold to 
commercial cattlemen. California and Arizona 
have been just about the best buyers of good 
quality breeding bulls in the entire country. 
There are many commercial operators who have 
graded up their breeding herds to a point where 
they are finding it extremely difficult to find 
breeding bulls that are actually good enough to 
continue the important work of breed improve- 
ment. Frankly, there are many people who are 
of the opinion that commercial cattlemen are 
making greater progress than are the registered 
beef cattle breeders, as a whole. Here is a real 
challenge to the registered beef cattle breeders. 
There are all too few genuine breeders such as 
Otto Battles, the sort of men who by instinct and 
by application are consistently breeding better anJ 
better cattle, the kind that will go out and sire 
calves that are better than their dams. Range cat- 
tlemen have learned to their sorrow that merely 
because a bull is registered in a breed association 
is not sufficient guarantee that he will prove to 
be a sire of good calves. Merely the fact that the 
sire and dam are both registered undoubtedly 
means that the offspring will be better than the 
offspring of mates of unknown heritage. But un- 
less the man who is mating sires and dams has 
a thorough understanding of what he is aiming 
to accomplish, it is doubtful if he may rightfully 
term himself a breeder and an improver. Over 
a period of many years, I have seen these Rose- 
mere cattle win more grand championships at 
Chicago and at other national shows than have 
been won by any other breeder within any breed. 

There are all too few men who own registered 
herds who are at the same time beef improvers. 
When a man like Otto Battles announces the sale 
of the Congdon & Battles herd, it is a matter 


that cannot but be viewed with deep regret yet 
this feeling is somewhat mitigated by the knowl- 
edge that these grand Aberdeen-Angus founda- 
tion cattle will be made available to other breed- 
ers throughout the country. There is no doubt 
as to the almost priceless value of these cattle 
to those who realize the responsibility that coes 
with the ownership of such stock. It is a genuine 
challenge to beef improvers. 


There is a vital need for beef improvement; 
vital need for the production, particularly, of good 
beef bulls that will add to the economic value of 
America’s vast range cattle business. Production 
of bulls suitable for range herd improvement is 
a profitable business from a financial standpoint, 
for we have the two elements needed for any suc- 
cessful business: a broad demand that will con- 
tinue to expand; and a limited supply that taxes 
the brains and abilities of breeders to increase 
sufficiently to meet the demand. But aside from 
the financial gains, which are all important, of 
course, is the reward which must come from the 
satisfaction of adding to the economic resources 
of our country. 


The live stock and meat business is said to be 
the biggest business in America; here in Cali- 
fornia one dollar out of every three received from 
all agricultural pursuits comes from live stock 
sales. In some of our western states, fully half 
of all agricultural cash income is gained from 
live stock sales and in Nevada, $11.00 out of $13.- 
00 received by agriculture comes from live stock 
sales! 


Breeders would do well to always consider the 
range trade and keep in mind the range needs. 
It is necessary for the range man to increase 
the percentage of calves raised per each 100 cows; 
he must obtain the greatest possible number of 
pounds of beef out of his available grass and 
forage; and he must constantly improve his prod- 
uct in order to obtain a sufficient premium in 
price to pay increased operation costs and still 
come out with a profit. He must have bulls with 
scale and substance, the kind that are good rust- 
lers, easy keepers and that possess natural flesh- 
ing qualities. 


That there is a tremendous field for develop- 
ment of Aberdeen-Angus breeding stock on the 
range goes without question, and especially is 
that true in California. Range men should be im- 
pressed with the fact that Aberdeen-Angus cattle 
have the substance and scale and that the off- 
spring, even when crossed on horned cattle, will 
be very nearly 100% naturally hornless. No cow- 
man likes to dehorn calves and yearlings; not 
only is it a laborious job but it is costly as well. 


More and more, production from range herds 
must be finished in feed lots here in the west. 
There is no doubt about the ability of Aberdeen- 
Angus calves to make profitable use out of grains 
and forage and to develop into beef fit for the 
most critical American consumer,
