Rosemere as I Saw It 
in 1907 


By W. J. KENNEDY 


Former Head of Animal Husbandry Dept. lowa State Col- 
lege, Ames, Iowa. Now Vice-President and General Man- 
ager, Anchor Serum Company, Kansas City, Missouri. 


HE old saying “Great oaks from little acorns grow” has been amply proven in the unprecedented 
© progress made in the Rosemere Aberdeen-Angus breeding establishment. This herd was es- 

tablished in a very modest way by Otto Battles’ mother in 1898. Mrs. Battles was operating just 
a good average Iowa farm. Like all good mothers she was deeply interested in her family. She was es- 
pecially interested in Otto. She wanted to make farm life worthwhile and attractive to him. He loved 
animals. She was a very practical woman. She believed the greatest rewards would come to those 
who studied and met the demands of the buying public. She could see a real future in the production 
of high class baby beef, thus her reason for selecting Aberdeen-Angus cattle. In the spring of 1907, 
while I was at the lowa State College, I received a letter from Mrs. Battles inviting me to spend a day 
at Rosemere. She wanted me to meet Otto and look over the Aberdeen-Angus breeding herd and es- 
pecialiy the show prospects which young Otto was fitting for the fall shows. She wanted me to frank- 
ly tell her and Otto as to whether or not their prospective show animals were good enough to take 
to the Iowa State Fair. 


I wrote Mrs. Battles that I would be at her home on a certain day. I will never forget that day. 
I found in Otto a bubbling-over young man who could talk and apparently think of nothing but Aber- 
deen-Angus cattle. I will never forget his dear, devoted mother, how interested and hopeful she was 
for him to make good. His sole ambition was to breed and own the best Aberdeen-Angus herd in this 
or any other country. It was a truly ambitious program. 


Both he and his mother wondered what chance a new and unknown exhibitor would have at our 
larger shows in competition with the nationally recognized herds. The name “McHenry” at that time 
stood at the very top in Aberdeen-Angus breeding circles. Otto wondered if he could ever show cattle 
good enough to beat the McHenry cattle. 


After spending several hours looking over the cattle and answering hundreds of questions I in- 
formed Otto and his mother that their show prospects looked good to me, that in my opinion they 
were good enough to go to the Iowa State Fair and the other leading State Fairs and stock shows. 
While I was handing out these encouraging words I kept asking myself the question—will he bring 
them along in good bloom or will he over-do them in his extreme anxiety to have them ready for 
their first appearance at a recognized show, the Iowa State Fair? The next time I saw the Rosemere 
Aberdeen-Angus cattle was at the Iowa State Fair where I was judging Aberdeen-Angus cattle. In 
the show ring young Otto was extremely nervous. He was very anxious to give each and every one of 
his animals a good showing. On the whole he did a good job. When I sent him to the top he would smile 
and sometimes when I sent other exhibitors over him tears could be seen rolling down his cheeks. It 
was a great day for Otto because he smiled much more often than he wept as he, a new and unknown 
exhibitor, had in many classes beaten the great McHenry animals and those shown by all the other ex- 
hibitors. That was his initial show and he had made good! 


The 1907 show circuit ended at the great International Show at Chicago where Otto Battles’ 
Rosemere herd won both Grand Champion honors in the Aberdeen-Angus breeding classes. From 1907 
to 1916 the Rosemere herd continued to make showring history. No other Aberdeen-Angus herd made a 
better record. In 1916 the Congdon & Battles partnership was formed. From that day to this the herd 
has grown in both point of numbers and very high individual excellence. For years we have all rec- 
ognized and truly admired the Rosemere type. In no other breeding establishment has a greater 
fixity of the desired type been found. It has been truly the work of perhaps America’s most con- 
structive and practical live stock breeder, Otto V. Battles, who started more than thirty years ago at 
the very bottom of the ladder and by his own initiative, studious attitude and persistence has now 
reached the pinnacle of success. 


What Otto V. Battles has done can be duplicated by other young men if they will work hard in 
studying breeding problems and ever keep in mind that live stock breedin 
those who can recognize the faults and deficiences in their own animals. 


& success comes only to 


Page 18 


Breed Builder 


By DR. C. D. LOWE 


Formerly a field representative for the Amer- 
ican Aberdeen-Angus Breeders’ Association, 
and now in charge of live stock extension work 
for the United States Department of Agricul- 
ture. 


“To make the best better”, the motto of 4-H Club 
work, well expresses the aims and activities of Otto V. 
Battles as a cattle breeder. This has always been and 
still is his major objective. How well he has succeeded 
is not a matter of opinion. Public records proclaim his 
success. 


My personal acquaintance with Mr. Battles and knowledge of his breeding oper- 
ations date back more than a quarter century. During this period my contacts have 
been made as a live stock development agent of a major railway system, as a state 
live stock extension specialist, as a field representative of the American Aberdeen- 
Angus Breeders’ Association, and in my present capacity. On my first visit to 
Rosemere I saw old “Quiet Lad”, his dam, Queen McHenry 47th, as well as many 
other foundation animals destined to make breed history, and was much impressed. 
Subsequent visits substantiated my original concept that here was a constructive 
breeder. As I travelled from one end of the country to the other I observed Rose- 
mere products in the hands of other breeders. It was significant that such ani- 
mals were proving to be so satisfactory to their owners, also to learn the high re- 
gard with which other breeders held Mr. Battles. 


While I was with the Association it was my privilege to place a good many cat- 
tle of Rosemere breeding in the hands of new owners. Among such animals was the 
ill-fated “Idolmere’’,, the bull shown to the grand championship of the 1919 ‘“Inter- 
national” by the French Broad Farms of Tennessee. 


Mr. Battles’ career as a breeder has been characterized by several qualities all 
too seldom encountered in his chosen profession. First, he started right by invest- 
ing his initial capital in only a few head of outstanding individuals which were to 
his liking, and as a consequence he never had to make a second start as so often is 
the case. Secondly, he has devoted himself to the task of constructive cattle breed- 
ing, to the practical exclusion of all other interests. Thirdly, he has consistently fol- 
lowed the system of exhibiting the products of his own herd, which may possibly 
best be expressed as “breeding his show cattle and showing his breeding cattle.” 
And fourthly, throughout his career, his original idea of combining the art and sci- 
ence of breeding has persisted. In his own words, this is expressed as “pick the 
right foundation stock, be able to recognize merit in the animals with which you 
work, select towards a proper type and blend blood lines in a manner that will pro- 
duce a maximum of good quality cattle and a minimum of poor ones.” . 


A man who has exhibited five International grand champion bulls and an equal 
number of females and has gained the admiration and respect of a great live stock in- 
dustry for his constructive breeding efforts and a reputation for fair and honorable 
dealing, needs no other endorsement. His works have spoken for him. Otto Battles, 
I salute you as a cattle breeder, as a gentleman, and as a friend. 


Page 19 


As the Herdsman 
Sees It 


By KALE WORKMAN 


Head Herdsman at Congdon & Battles 1936, 1937, 1938 and one of the 
best known fitters and showmen of International winners in America. 


\HIS is a sad time to write for it falls to my 
(CH lot to help tear down the lifetime work of one 
of the most patient men J have ever known. Only a 
few days ago we culled out of the sale offering ten 
old cows, some eighteen, some nineteen, and one that 
was twenty-one years old. To some people they 
would be just old cows that had outlived their useful- 
ness but to our “Chief” they were loyal pals, ‘“‘pion- 
eers’”’, he called them. Mr. Battles said: ‘These old 
friends came to Yakima shortly after Idid. There weren’t many registered 
cattle of any kind here in the northwest then, but they were an example for 
cattlemen to see that the blacks could take it”. 

Those cows were real pioneers, too, for I know of no Aberdeen-Angus herds 
in the west that were founded without a good percent of Rosemere blood 
or else a visit to the herd here at Yakima inspired the rancher to try Aberdeen- 
Angus. Those ten old cows in the last fifteen years have left in the neighbor- 
hood of 3,000 direct descendants—eall halt of them bulls, just think how many 
black calves that many bulls could sire, and you can begin to see what 
tosemere has done for the Aberdeen-Angus breed in the west. For the west 
has used most of the cattle that Rosemere has produced—they like our type, 
Se ey bone, straight backs and legs, plenty of scale—quality and size com- 
bined. 

I had worked for Rosemere almost a year before I fully realized the plan, 
policy and wisdom of Mr. Battles’ method of selecting and presenting his show 
cattle to the public. We were picking calves for the show herd one day and our 
opinions differed. Then he explained to me that any calf that went into the 
show barn must be bred right. Both their sires and dams must have come from 
good producing strains and must have produced good cattle and the cross should 
be proven good. He explained to me that he would rather show just a good in- 
dividual that he knew had the back ground to make his future owner money, 
than a sensational winner that wouldn’t get one good calf out of a hundred. 

Our show cattle are fed with the samie viewpoint in mind. Nothing is “pam- 
pered”. The selected calves are brought to the barn with their dams and re- 
ceive all the grain they want. Those whose dams are poor milkers or old cows 
are put on nurse cows. We only have twelve nurse cows so you can see no calf 
gets two cows to “make” him. Our calves are never ag fat as some of our 
competitors but they are well developed and it is easy enough to select the type 
for yearlings and two year olds. The greatest thing about this system is that 
no calf is over-done nor do we have any trouble breeding them when they are 
long yearlings although I’ve had some bulls and cows just as fat as the next 
fellow’s in the show ring. I think that show yard phrase “he could win if he 
was just fat enough” is getting the breed nowhere fast. Whenever I hear it I 
think of what my dad told me at the American Royal when I told him Eppon- 
ian 8th could win if he was “just a little fatter”. He said, “Another year of de- 
velopment will make him perfect, but I can’t see why fat should make him win!” 

To me it has been more than just a “job” working here with all these good 
cattle under the capable and efficient hands of Mr. Battles. It has been a liber- 
al education and I can truthfully say that I wouldn’t trade my three years here 
at Rosemere for four years in any college. I hope everybody who really ap- 
preciates good live stock will study the pedigrees in this catalog and then come 
to Maquoketa in ample time before the sale to study the individuals and see 
these masterpieces of a master breeder.

importation, with 
later shipments, also 
from the old country, 
formed the founda- 
tion of the herds 


which were estab- 

HAROLD K. CASTLE, well known steamship owner, sugar producer and Aberdeen-Angus breeder in lished here in Ha- 
the Hawaiian Islands. Also an internationally known polo player. Mr. Castle is shown above with tase 

some of his prize winning dogs and some members of his Aberdeen-Angus herd. wail in_ those early 

days. Importations 


from the United 
States started in 1894 since which time breeding stock has been brought in pretty consistently off and on up 
to the present time. For a number of years now, as you know, shipments have come in considerable numbers 
and almost yearly in more recent times. 


We have been breeding Aberdeen-Angus on my Kaneohe Ranch since the early nineties and while quite 
a number of years ago experiments were made in crossing with other breeds, we came back to the Aberdeen- 
Angus and for about twenty years have used nothing but pure bred bulls on this ranch. For many years prac- 
tically all of the importations have been made from your herd, as you know, and it has been not only a source 
of great satisfaction but a keen pleasure to have done business with you and I am sure that all others that 
have had dealings with you here feel exactly the way I do. 


I cannot tell you how sorry I feel that it becomes necessary to disperse the herd. You have, through 
years of effort and intelligent operation, built up a wonderful establishment and it is a great loss to all con- 
structive breeders to have such a foundation unit given up. This, of course, is not as bad as it sounds, as 
you will undoubtedly continue the valuable and constructive work you have been doing, in new fields where 
I certainly join with all others in wishing you very best success. With kindest regards and aloha from us all, 
Sincerely, (Signed) Harold. 


More Aberdeen-Angus are 
Needed in the West 


By J. SHERMAN GUTTRIDGE 


Of Guttridge Bros.—Extensive commercial operators of Prairie City, 
Oregon who also have a select pure bred herd of Aberdeen-Angus. 


The dispersion of the Congdon & Battles herd of Aberdeen-Angus will be 
a serious loss to the entire country, but especially to the breeders on the Pa- 
cific Coast. Congdon & Battles were the first breeders of any consequence to 
establish a herd in the west, and have played the leading part in getting Ab- 
erdeen-Angus established in the western states. For many years the Rose- 
mere Herd has been exhibited at all western live stock shows, and it is 
through these exhibitions that a great many breeders have first come into 
contact with the Aberdeen-Angus breed. Many range live stock operators 
have secured their bulls repeatedly from Congdon & Battles and always knew 
they could get high quality bulls from them. A large part of western cow- 
men do not need enough bulls to justify going into the middle West and with 
the dispersion of Congdon & Battles these breeders will be materially affected. That part of the United 
States west of the Rocky Mountains is one oi the great cattle producing areas. It is this section of the coun- 
try that will see a great demand for Aberdeen-Angus in the future. There are few Aberdeen-Angus in the 
west in comparison with the other beef breeds, and unless there are established new breeding plants, numer- 
ous range breeders will not continue to breed Aberdeen-Angus for the reason that. they cannot get new bulls. 


There should be no reason to lose any range herds now using Aberdeen-Angus bulls if the Aberdeen- 
Angus Association will devote more time and effort to promote the breed in this section of the country. 
There is no reason why the breed should not continue to gain popularity in the west, but this means that new 
herds are needed to carry on from where Congdon & Battles leave off.

To My Aberdeen-Angus 
Friends 


By FRED HAHNE, Editor and Publisher 


Aberdeen-Angus Journal, Webster City, Iowa 


TTO V. BATTLES has requested that I write some- 
thing for publication in this splendid memorial to 
the passing of one of the greatest and one of the most 
important partnership arrangements that has had to do 
with the promotion of Aberdeen-Angus cattle since my 
acquaintance with the breed and the men who are respon- 
sible for its present day high standing in the beef making 
world. Otto refused to assign a subject. Therefore what I shall say here is of my 
own invention and not subject to blue penciling before publication. 


To me, Otto V. Battles has been a friend. When I say, friend, I mean just that 
—a comrade, true blue, four square in dealings of every nature, a man that I have 
admired all these years because of his determination to do things, his tenacity 
and his modesty upon accomplishment. What I say for Otto V. Battles I can likewise 
say for his partner, Walter Congdon. To men like these the breed owes much of 
its stability, its influence, its growing favoritism among cattlemen everywhere. It 
has been my privilege to traverse east and west as well as north and south—all but 
three or four of the states and provinces of Canada. Everywhere is found the influ- 
ence of this herd. Scarcely can a man travel a day north of the Mexican iine with- 
out finding a Rosemere bull or a group of Rosemere bred females, and invariably the 
reports are good. That’s a record that can only be compared with the show yard 
career of this great herd. 


Well do I recall a day when Otto V. Battles turned to me in the International 
ring after an animal that he had hoped was good enough to win the purple had been 
turned down, and remarked, “It just about takes the heart out of a fellow to see his 
year’s work deprived of its just due in the finals. But then, he was right in there 
fighting for it anyway.” My reply to him then and there was simply this: “Otto, 
you have the greatest show record of any man on this continent. While you do not 
win, perhaps, as many championships as you would like to win, your cattle are among 
the last contenders more often than the cattle of any other breeder that I know.” 
This is the one thing that to me has stood out above all the grand championships 
that it has been my pleasure to report these last eighteen years. Rosemere cattle 
have met all who have come and gone during these years, holding always that cher- 
ished place of final contender. It is to the men who have made this partnership 
possible that I wish to hand the roses today. 


FROM FARM BOY TO FAME 
(Concluded from page 14) 


ed the entire United States and Canada. 

In conclusion let us mention an extraordinary hon- 
or that was bestowed upon Mr. Battles in 1929 from 
Scotland. He was invited to judge at the great an- 
nual Perth show and sale, where some 800 animals 
are entered. I am under the impression that this 
was the first case of its kind on record. At any 
rate it constituted high recognition of splendid work 
done with Aberdeen-Angus, this side the sea, and 
resulted in the formation of personal friendship with 
leading Scottish and English breeders that are still 
kept alive by correspondence. Otto Battles has con- 
tributed at least as much to the advancement of pub- 
lic interest in pure bred live stock as any other 
American of his generation. 

It seems little short of a calamity that such a his- 
tory-making herd as this should be dispersed; and 
yet, who knows? Considering the interests of the 
breed as a whole, it may well be that the distribu- 


tion of such valuable and highly concentrated ma- 
terial throughout the American Aberdeen-Angus 
body may serve as a solid basis for the mainten- 
ance, or even advancement, of existing high breed 
standards. Many a herd now in the formative stage 
—especially if it be composed of more or less diver- 
gent blood elements—may be started on the upward 
way by the selection of a good, strongly-bred Rose- 
mere bull; and those who are in a position to take 
on a sizeable group of the Congdon & Battles fe- 
males, along with such a bull, can be well assured 
of carrying on without abatement the established 
Rosemere character and quality. Opportunity, it 
is said, knocks at least once at every man’s door. The 
event which this publication is designed to commem- 
orate would seem to call for recognition at the hands 
of every individual in North American having at 
heart the well-being, the continued prosperity of the 
Aberdeen-Angus breed. 


(End of Contributed Articles)

Pictorial Section 


Congdon & Battles in the West 


By OTTO V. BATTLES 


LTHOUGH the Rosemere Herd, from which the Congdon & Battles Herd mainly originated, had 
its beginning in lowa—having been started there by my mother—the breeding operations of Cong- 
don & Battles began in the fertile Yakima Valley of Washington State early in 1916. My origin- 

al partner, the late Chester A. Congdon of Duluth, Minnesota, had in January of that year purchased 
a number of the best animals from my Rosemere herd as well as several from two other breeders of 
the middle west, for the purpose of establishing a herd on his ranch in the Yakima Valley. A few weeks 
later Mr. Congdon and I entered into an agreement whereby our two herds would be merged and I would 
assume complete supervision of the business. , ; 

I recall as though it were only yesterday, how during our discussion of the details of this pro- 
posed partnership Mr. Congdon stated very emphatically that it was his ambition that we develop the 
greatest herd of Aberdeen-Angus cattle in the world and that he felt that I was capable of doing it. I 
had the same ambition and to achieve it I have striven hard during the subsequent years. But be that 
as it may, whatever has been accomplished has been due in no small measure to the cooperation I re- 
ceived in the beginning from Mr. Congdon and after his death, from his heirs. This has been particu- 
larly true of his son, Walter, who is well known in the Aberdeen-Angus fraternity and who has always 
taken an active interest in the herd. 

Mr. Congdon was a true pioneer. He felt that the far northwest and the whole west, for that mat- 
ter, offered a promising field for the Doddie and that Congdon & Battles should be the first to cultivate 
it. I agreed with him. So we decided to tackle the venture together. We would introduce a new breed 
in a totally new field and we would overcome the obstacles which we knew faced us by developing a 
great herd, a large herd, and one which would be known around the world for its outstanding quality. 
We would scatter over this vast western cattle empire Aberdeen-Angus of approved type and confor- 
mation—cattle that would compare favorably with the superior type of Hereford with which the entire 
west was populated. 

As I look back now I realize how strenuous the job has been. Unfounded and unfavorable stories 
which had been circulated for years about the supposed deficiencies of Aberdeen-Angus cattle could not 
be discredited over night nor by any kind of magic. We had actually to prove to the western range pro- 
ducer that contrary to what he had been told, Aberdeen-Angus cattle were hardy, good rustlers and pro- 
lific—that they came by these qualities inherently and that Aberdeen-Angus bulls would hold their own 
on the open range even with bulls of the horned breeds. In other words, we had to demonstrate by 
sheer merit that Aberdeen-Angus cattle were the most profitable cattle for the range man to produce. 

Now that our labors here in behalf of the breed are about at an end, I try to visualize in my own 
mind whether or not they have been successful—whether we have really accomplished the things we so 
enthusiastically visioned in 1916. At first thought, the answer is no, because we have not yet come any- 
where near giving the breed the place it deserves in this great western cattle country. But in a more 
optimistic mood I consider the other side of the question and I am encouraged. I recall how when I first 
came to the west I was told on every hand by people who were familiar with conditions here that they 
wished me luck in introducing my breed but that they thought that I was butting my head against a 
stone wall because Aberdeen-Angus cattle were not suited to this country, particularly to range condi- 
tions, etc. And I recall my reply, which was that I did not believe the people of the west had had an op- 
portunity to properly study Aberdeen-Angus cattle and that they, therefore, were not familiar with the 
fact that Aberdeen-Angus are inherently the hardiest of all cattle—that they had to rustle in the rugged 
hills of Scotland, where they originated, in order to exist, and that it was my purpose to demonstrate these 
and other superior qualities to the people of the west to the end that these characteristics would be recog- 
nized and accepted. I recall that for a number of years the Congdon & Battles show herds campaigned 
the western circuits as the only Aberdeen-Angus cattle on exhibition—covering in the beginning shows 
without even an Aberdeen-Angus classification and without prize money for Aberdeen-Angus cattle; 
and how our cattle were erroneously referred to as from this or that cross with everything from a Gallo- 
way to a buffalo. They were most frequently referred to, however, as Galloways. This breed had been 
used some in the west but apparently without gaining friends, if the chance conversations we over- 
heard amongst fair goers were to be given any weight. But gradually the picture changed. These same 
Congdon & Battles show herds were going east and winning at such important places as Kansas City 
and Chicago. Surely there must be something to these cattle after all. Perhaps it would be yood busi- 
ness to give them a trial. And so the idea took hold and began slowly to spread. Aberdeen-Angus cat- 
tle began to be recognized for what they really were. We were putting our bulls on the range and where- 
ever they went they made friends to the end that it wasn’t long before our demand from this source 
outgrew our ability to supply it and we brought in numerous shipments of bulls from the middle west 
for our customers, and others bought directly from there. This condition encouraged the starting of 
other pure bred herds in the west so we began to supply pure bred breeding stock—a trade which has 
steadily grown until today there are a great many pure bred herds in the west which were established 
with a Congdon & Battles foundation. During the time we have been in business here we have actually 
bred and sold over 5000 Aberdeen-Angus cattle ourselves, not to mention the thousands that have been 
spread over the west from other herds which were started with Congdon & Battles cattle. So, after all 
I like to at least give myself the satisfaction of feeling that our pioneering in the west has borne fruit. 
In fact, I believe the results are now becoming acutely apparent. I know that our own business reflects 
this condition, The many fine letters I have received from western people since our dispersion sale has 
become known registering regret, disappointment, fear for the future without our leadership, etc., make 
me feel like a deserter from the ranks. I know, of course, however, that there are others who will 
carry the work on and I hope that some amongst them will take up where Congdon & Battles leave off 
and lead the way as we have tried to do toward an Aberdeen-Angus empire on the Pacific Coast It is os 
green field and one which holds unlimited possibilities. In making this statement I am not foolin my- 
self by believing that Aberdeen-Angus cattle are going to crowd the Hereford entirely off the a x 
This breed has been dominant in the range country too long for anything like that to happen. But there 
is a place for both breeds and for Aberdeen-Angus to be a success it is not necessary that the bi oped 
range owners change over to Aberdeen-Angus. Some, of course, have done this and others will ea the 
really big field for the Aberdeen-Angus in the west lies with the small range owner and the tho da 
of farm owners such as are found in the middle west and similar cattle producing sections With the : 
two classes of producers the Doddie is becoming increasingly popular and they alone assu : ese 
ing future for the breed in this part of the country. ; eee eee

Herd Bulls 


PRIZEMERE 32d—Was accorded the distinction of being the 
greatest sire of his time. He was not only a great sire but a 
prolific one. The majority of the animals in this dispersion are 
by Prizemere 32d, one reason for this being that his offspring 
were the kind we held back to build up the herd and carry on 
the Rosemere tradition, and the other reason being that he was 
such a sure sire. This characteristic of being a sure sire of 
good ones, breeds on in his offspring. A perusal of the Inter- 
national winnings of his get convinces us that he was the great- 
est sire ever used in the Rosemere Herd—or in any herd, for 
that matter. BLACKMERE 97th—A Chicago International first prize winner 

in 1930, this good son of Prizemere 32d out of the great show 

cow, Blackbird of Rosemere 56th, has been a consistent pro-

His dam, Barbara of Rosemere 64th, was Junior and Reserve Grand Champion Female as a junior calf at the Chicago Interna- 
tional of 1928 and her dam, Barbara of Rosemere 40th, was Junior Champion as a summer calf at the Chicago International of 
1924. Thus we have an unbroken line of three generations of International Champions and this is not an isolated case amongst 
Rosemere pedigrees by any means. We were unfortunate in losing Barbarian of Rosemere 64th through death before he had seen 
much service in the herd. 

But after all, we realize that our cattle themselves which you will see at sale time, must furnish the standard by which you 
will determine whether or not our breeding plan has been a success and as far as we are concerned we are willing to stand or 
fall on your judgment at that time. We are anxious that you should understand, however, that the major portion of these cattle 
will be presented at sale time in just their everyday working clothes. In other words, the breeding herd has not received any 
special fitting for the occasion. Of course there are some show animals and show prospects in the sale and these have been car- 
ried on full feed for some time with the idea of preparing them for this year’s shows. They will be in the proper condition at sale 
time to go to the early shows and give good accounts of themselves.

6th Prize group 3 steers in 1927........ Congdon & Battles 


O make comparisons easier and to facilitate the movement of animals through the ring 
we have adopted a group plan of cataloging and selling. The various groups will be 


stabled or penned together at the sale grounds. By careful sorting we have, to the best of our 
ability, put together in the different groups the animals which are most uniform in breeding 
and conformation. The animals of each group will be sold separately but all of those of one 


group will be sold before any from another group enter the ring.

CONGDON & BATTLES DISPERSION, JUNE 20, 21, 1938 


CN Uli olO.W GAT ELE 


HIS group constitutes the entire show herd. Included in it ar i i 
© hibited with success at the major shows of the country, aerate a nance Pena the 
younger animals which have been under preparation for this year’s shows. All of these show nite i‘ 
and show prospects have had the same fitting which we always give our show cattle. In other words tied 
will carry the same condition at sale time as our own show cattle have always carried at that time of the 
year. They will, therefore, be ready to go to the early shows and render a good account of themselves 

Amongst the older animals that have already been shown are such outstanding individuals as Prizemere 
387th, Lot 14, undefeated two year old at all of the major shows of 1937 except the Chicago International 
where he stood second to the Grand Champion Bull of the show, which was also from a Rosemere dam owned 
by Edwards Bros. of Ontario, Canada. Other great bulls in this group are Epponian 9th and Epponian 11th 
of Rosemere—full brothers to each other and full brothers to Epponia C. B. 2d, the dam of our 1936 Inter- 
national Grand Champion Bull, Epponian 8th of Rosemere. Both of these Epponian bulls should be decided 
attractions from the standpoint of show bulls and outstanding herd sire prospects. Epponian 9th will show 
in the three year old class this year and Epponian 11th will be a senior yearling. 

Prizemere 443d, another member of this group, is by Eileenmere 61st—already the sire of International 
champions. Prizemere 443d gave an excellent account of himself in the summer calf class at all of the big 
shows last year and is a good prospect for the senior yearling class this year. Another star attraction in this 
offering is the summer calf, Queenmere 299th, sired by the International Grand Champion of 1933, Barbar- 
ian of Rosemere 64th. Queenmere 299th is an extremely promising young bull both from the standpoint Olea 
pew bull and a great herd sire. There are many other fine bulls in this group, but we must pass on to the 
emales. 

Here we find the great young show cow, Blackcap of Rosemere 112th, which has been a successful mem- 
ber of our show herd for the past two years and which is the best prospect that we know of for the cow 
class this year—one of the best prospects we have ever fitted, for that matter. She is a daughter of that 
eo kg of show animals, EKileenmere 61st. 

ree more outstanding young females in this group by Hileenmere 61st are Barbara of 

Irene of Rosemere 53d, and Novice 28th of Rosemere. Barbara of Rosemere 170th was hard Be heat ees 
in the senior calf class and is much better now—a great prospect we think for the senior yearling class this 
year. She is what you might call a “chip off the old block” because she is typical of the Rosemere Barbaras 
which in recent years have produced more winners than any other one strain in the Rosemere Herd. In con- 
nection with this, it is interesting to note that in this particular group there is one daughter of Barbara of 
Rosemere 64th, three granddaughters of Barbara of Rosemere 64th and one daughter of a full sister to Bar- 
bara of Rosemere 64th. As will be remembered, Barbara of Rosemere 64th was the Chicago International 
Junior and Reserve Grand Champion female in 1928 and was also the dam of Barbarian of Rosemere 64th, 
the 1933 Chicago International Grand Champion bull. The Rosemere Barbara family as well as the other 
great Rosemere producing strains, is a convincing demonstration of the way Rosemere animals perpetuate 
the characteristics of their ancestors which were great show and producing animals in their own time. 

Another outstanding attraction amongst the show females is Ectessa 4th which will show in the aged cow 
class this year. She is a full sister to the famous sire, Eileenmere 61st and is with calf to the service of Ep- 
ponian 9th of Rosemere, the same combination of breeding which produced the 1936 International Grand 
Champion, Epponian 8th of Rosemere. There are numerous other attractive females in this group which will 
be appreciated when they are seen at sale time.

CONGDON & BATTLES DISPERSION, JUNE 20, 21, 1938 Page 57 


GROUP IV—BREEDING COWS 


these twenty we were almost surprised ourselves with the outstanding excellence of the group in every 
particular. In our opinion it is doubtful if twenty better cows of any breed could be assembled in 
America or anywhere else and a prominent Aberdeen-Angus authority who recently visited us from the east 
expressed the same opinion. Let us point out just a few outstanding members of the group. For marae 
there are two daughters (Lots 63 and 66) and one granddaughter (Lot 65) of Pride of Rosemere 67th, the 1926 
International Grand Champion Female; two daughters (Lots 55 and 56) of Eileenmere 15th, the 1929 Inter- 


(1) are used to seeing a lot of good cows together in the Rosemere pastures, but after we had selected 


national Grand Champion Bull; and one daughter (Lot 64) of Prizemere 101st, the sire of Barbarian of Rose- 
mere 64th, International Grand Champion Bull of 1933. This cow is also a sister on the dam’s side to the 
great two year old show bull of the offering, Prizemere 387th (Lot 14). Then there are two daughters (Lots 
54 and 66) of the champion sire, Hileenmere 61st and eleven by that greatest of all Rosemere sires, Prizemere 
32d, the recent premier sire of the breed. Amongst these daughters of Prizemere 32d is Epponia C. B. 2d 
(Lot 58), dam of the great International Grand Champion Bull of 1936, Epponian 8th of Rosemere, and she 
will be due to calve before sale day to Hileenmere 61st, sire of the 1936 International Grand Champion Bull. 
In other words, Epponia C. B. 2d should have by her side sale day a full brother or full sister to Epponian 8th 
of Rosemere.

GROUP V-—-BREEDING COWS 


HIS group might easily be rated as the most distinguished lot of the whole offering, due to the fact that ev- 
ery cow in it is sired by that greatest of all Rosemere sires, Prizemere 32d. Individually they compare fav- 
orably with the group just preceding them in this catalog and as producers they have no superiors in the 

herd. Among the individuals in this group will be found such cows as Barbara of Rosemere 93d, a full sister to 
the International Champion and champion producing Barbara of Rosemere 64th; and Barbara of Rosemere ILA, 2 
daughter of Barbara of Rosemere 64th. Then there is Blackbird of Rosemere 169th a three quarters sister to our 
recent two year old show cow, Blackbird of Rosemere 164th, the 1936 American Royal Grand Champion cow. Black- 
cap of Rosemere 83d of this group is a full sister to Blackcapmere 62d, Reserve Junior Champion bull at the 1929 
Chicago International. Eileen of Rosemere 17th is a direct descendant of the International Grand Champion cow, 
Eileen Lass—which was the foundation cow of all the great Eileenmeres. There are also in this group two great 
granddaughters (Lots 82 and 83) of Rita Pride, one of the great Pride foundation cows of the herd through which 


many great show cattle have descended.

GROUP VI—OLDER BREEDING COWS 


HIS group of seventeen comprises some of the older cows of the herd but we believe they are 
none the less valuable. They are by the sires which really laid the foundation for the great- 


© 


ness of Rosemere. Amongst them will be found probably the only living d 


of the 1919 Chicago International. One of these is Idessa of Rosemere 4th, a c 
variably has twin calves and she should calve again before sale time. Probably 


of these daughters is none other than the 1926 International Grand Champion Fe 


Rosemere 25th has produced many outstanding winners. 


of Rosemere breeding, Oakville Quiet Lad, and this cow still has the appearance of an animal in her 
prime. In this group there are also two daughters of Meraman of Tierra Alta, the greatest outcross sire 
ever used in the herd, and sire of the famous Prizemere 9th. One of these daughters of Meraman, Kind- 
ness C. B. 2d, is the dam of Prizemere 138th, First Prize Senior Bull Calf at the Chicago International 
of 1929. She is also the dam of Pride of Rosemere 289th, First Prize Senior Heifer Calf and Junior 
Champion Female at the Chicago International of 1932. Kindness C. B. 2d has a bull calf now by 
Fileenmere 157th which will sell at her side sale day and he is a real show prospect. In this group will 
be found, also, three daughters of Idolmere 4th, a full brother to Idolmere, the Grand Champion Bull 


tractions of this group, however, are two daughters of Prizemere 9th, the bull which for the number 
of calves he sired produced the highest percentage of International winners of any bull of the breed. One 


mere 67th (a full sister to the premier sire, Prizemere 32d) and the other one is Blackcap of Rose- 
mere 25th, the dam of Blackcapmere 113th, 1937 American Royal Grand Champion Bull. Blackcap of 


aughter of the father

GROUP VII—FOUR YEAR OLD HEIFERS 


HIS group, which is the largest group in the offering, is made up of the heifers we have been holding 
back for the past several years to eventually replace the older breeding cows and they are a very se- 
lect lot in both breeding and individuality. For instance, twenty-three of them are daughters of the 

premier sire, Prizemere 32d; and the remainder are by such famous Rosemere sires as Eileenmere 61st 
Queenmere 70th and Blackmere 97th. Most of these heifers are pasture bred to Eileenmere 157th, good breed- 
ing son of the grand champion, Eileenmere 85th, and out of one of the Tolan Evergreen cows. Eileenmere 
157th was purchased two years ago from the Tolan herd to be used as one of our chief herd sires. We don’t 
know that we can say anything more for these heifers than that we have kept them back to carry forward 
the Rosemere banner and that we feel every one of them is capable of doing just that. 


Owned by Congdon & Battles, Yakima, Washington.

GROUP VII—THE COMING TWO YEAR OLD HEIFERS 


HE heifers making up this group are the pick of our 1936 calf crop. The less desirable heifers of this age 
have all been sold privately. Group VIII is a wonderfully uniform lot—in fact they are typical of all Rose- 
mere cattle in this respect. They are sired by such well known Rosemere sires as Hileenmere 61st, Queen- 


© 


mere 70th, Blackmere 97th, Blackcap Eileenmere 3d, and Ericmere 60th, and there is also one heifer by Prizemere 
32d, and she is a full sister to the outstanding two year old show bull in the offering, Prizemere 387th. We feel 
that the young heifers in this group should be particularly desirable as foundation females. 


They represent 
the very best blood of the Rosemere Herd. We have never produced anything better in breeding and as to individ- 
uality they will speak for themselves when they are seen at sale time. As an example of the breeding back of 
these heifers let us point out Barbara of Rosemere 173d, a daughter of Barbara of Rosemere 49th, which was a full 
sister to Barbara of Rosemere 47th, the dam of the 1937 Chicago International Grand Champion Bull, Bandolier of 
Anoka 6th shown by Edwards Bros. Most of these heifers are pasture bred to the young Kileenmere 61st bull, Black- 
mere 195th, which we consider an outstanding breeding pro spect. He is from the same line of Blackbirds on the dam’s 
side that has produced all of the great Blackbird show winners and producers in the Rosemere Herd.

Page 82 CONGDON & BATTLES DISPERSION, JUNE 20, 21, 1938 


GROWPR TIX ELE E RaGEGY BS 


HIS group is made up of 1937 heifer calves. Although the last group to be cataloged, they are by no 

means the least desirable group in the sale. In fact, it is our honest opinion that they will average 
S as high in quality as any group heretofore mentioned. In other words, at the same age of some of the 
other groups we feel that they will compare favorably even with what we consider our No. 1 group of cows in 
the sale, and they come naturally by their high quality. Most of them are sired by two of Rosemere’s great- 
est present day sires, Eileenmere 61st and Queenmere 70th. This is a beautiful lot of heifers. A recent east- 
ern visitor really liked them better than any other group in the offering. They are potential producers of 
champions.

Final Tribute to Prizemere 32d 


After finishing compiling this catalog we felt 
that the outstanding achievements of this great 
Rosemere sire as revealed by this book, entitle 
him to the honor of being designated as Rose- 
mere’s King of Sires! 


Page 90 CONGDON & BATTLES DISPERSION, JUNE 20, 21, 1938 


10 the Recipient of 
this Cald/0l 


I have just spent a week with Manager Otto V. Battles at the Yakima 
farms checking carefully the cattle that are listed on the preceding pages. 
Together we rode the pastures from end to end and met individualiy every 
animal listed in this catalog, making careful observations as we went. 
They made a deep and lasting impression upon me as Otto told the indi- 
vidual history of each anima! as we came to it. I do not hesitate to recom- 
mend this offering as the greatest collection of Aberdeen-Angus breeding 
cattle ever assembled in one sale on this continent. 


It was indeed a pleasure and a coveted privilege to see the great herd 
bulls in their respective roomy paddocks, as they appeared in their work- 
ing togs, great, massive, thick-fleshed individuals—the very picture of 
Rosemere accomplishments. It was our first time to meet the great lot 
of matrons, stately and majestic (except, of course, those which had grac- 
ed the show rings of: this nation). These priceless gems are soon to be 
yours. The pictorial section of this catalog will tell you more at a glance 
than I could possibly convey on pages. I just want to say to the prospect- 
ive future owners of the wonderful cattle that I am planning to be at the 
famous Rosemere Farms at Maquoketa, Iowa, at least one or two days be- 
fore the sale opens for the exclusive purpose of being of service to any 
who may wish to go over them prior to the sale. If I can be of service to 
you in helping to make selections, do not hesitate to make that desire 
known. Dick Hahne and Gay Quammen, also of the Journal staff, will be 
on hand to assist—all three of us will be at your service. 


I was delighted and impressed beyond expectation with the quality, 
type, superlative conformation, size, scale, condition and breed character 
revealed as these stately matrons and their sons and daughters fed in the 
various pastures. These cattle are being presented in just their regular 
breeding condition, ready to go anywhere and carry on successfully, just 
the condition in which Congdon & Battles have kept them during their 
life time—not pampered, but well cared for, and I believe that they will fit 
into their new homes satisfactorily and with distinction. 


The thing that impresses me most of all is the very fact that there 
will be enough good cattle to go around. Perhaps there will be unexpect- 
ed opportunities to procure bargains—there always are in events of even 
less magnitude—and for that reason I wish that every buyer might re- 
main constantly at the ringside in order to take advantage of such an oc- 
casion should it arise. May we have the pleasure of helping show you 
these cattle? 


Sincerely, | 


Aberdeen-Angus Journal 


The Fred Hahne Printing Co., Webster City, Iowa.
